> आस्मै ।  
> सं । वि॑व्य॒े । इंद्रः ।॑ वृ॒जनं॒ । न । भूम ।॒ भर्ति॑ । स्व॒धावान् ।॑ ओप॒शंइ॑व॒ । द्यां ॥६॥

##### ॥ सायणभाष्यम् ॥ (Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya)

> यद्य इंद्रो महिना महत्त्वेन स्वैश्वर्येण नृभ्यो नेतृभ्यः कर्मनिर्वाहकेभ्यो यजमानेभ्यः प्रास्ति प्रभवति ईष्टे स्वर्गादिप्रदाने रक्षितुं अस्मै रक्षकायेंद्राय रोदसी द्यावापृथिव्यौ कक्ष्ये कक्ष्ययते सती संचाराय नारं नालं । न
> पर्याप्ते । जगतोऽधीशस्येंद्रस्य संचारायाल्पत्वाद्दावापृथिव्यौ न पर्याप्ते
> इत्यर्थः । सृष्टादपि स्रष्टुर्महिम्मोऽधिकत्वादिति भावः । आयमिंद्रो वृजनं न भूम
> भूमिं भूतानि वा वृजनं न वृजनमिव ॥ वृजेः कर्तनार्थस्येदं रूपं ॥ तदिव
> तद्यथावृणोति तथायमसि स्वतेजसा सं विस्वे । समृगावृणोति लोकत्रयं ।
> किंचायं स्वधावानन्नवान् वृष्टिलक्षणोदकवान्वा द्यां दिनं भर्ति । भिभर्ति । धारणे
> दृष्टांतः । ओपशमिव । ईषदुपशेत इत्योपशं शृंगं । तद्वृषभ इव । यद्वा ।
> परस्परं समीपे वर्तमानं क्षित्यंतरिक्षाख्यं लोकद्वयमोपशं। तदिव द्यां बिभर्ति ।
> लोकत्रयमसि बिभर्तित्यर्थः ॥ बृञ्चो लट छांदसः शपो लुक् ॥

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

Sāyaṇa says that when this Indra, by his greatness, by his own lordship, is able over the leaders, the sacrificers who carry out the rites, to bestow heaven and the like and to protect them, for this protecting Indra the two world-halves, heaven and earth, though treated as girths, are not sufficient for his movement. That is, since they are too small for the movement of Indra, the overlord of the world, heaven and earth do not suffice; the sense is that the creator's greatness exceeds even that of what is created. This Indra encompasses the earth or the beings, like a वृजनं (*vṛjanaṃ*), an enclosure; the word is a form of the root *vṛj* in the sense of cutting (**NOTE**). As that encloses, so he with his own brilliance encloses the three worlds. Moreover, this Indra, possessing स्वधा (*svadhā*), food, or water in the form of rain, bears the sky, the day, भर्ति (*bharti*), that is, बिभर्ति (*bibharti*). The example for bearing is ओपशमिव (*opaśamiva*): that which lies slightly upon something is ओपशं (*opaśaṃ*), a horn; as a bull bears it. Or else: the two worlds called earth and atmosphere, lying close to each other, are ओपशं (*opaśaṃ*); like that he bears the sky; the meaning is that he bears the three worlds.

##### प्रतिपदार्थ (Pratipadārtha)

- यत् (*yat*)—which Indra
- इत्या (*ityā*)—truly
- महिना (*mahinā*)—by his greatness (lordship)
- वृभ्यः (*vṛbhyaḥ*)—to the leaders (the sacrificers who carry out the rites)
- प्र आस्ति (*pra āsti*)—becomes able to bestow fruits such as heaven, or to protect
- अस्मै (*asmai*)—for this (protecting) Indra
- कक्ष्ये (*kakṣye*)—those that are like girths
- रोदसी (*rodasī*)—earth and atmosphere
- न अरं (*na araṃ*)—are not wide enough (for his movement)
- इंद्रः (*iṃdraḥ*)—this Indra
- भूम (*bhūma*)—the earth
- वृजनं न (*vṛjanaṃ na*)—as the sky encloses
- सं विव्ये (*saṃ vivye*)—(with his brilliance, the three worlds) he pervades
- (किंच आयं (*kiṃca āyaṃ*)—moreover

### Printed p. 271

- स्वधावान् (*svadhāvān*)—endowed with food (water in the form of rain)
- ओपशं इव (*opaśaṃ iva*)—as a bull bears a horn; or, as he also upholds the earth and the atmosphere, which lie close to each other
- भर्ति (*bharti*)—upholds (is the support)

##### भावार्थ (Bhāvārtha)

Indra, the protector and the supremely glorious one of the sacrificers who carry out the rites, surpasses heaven and earth. As the sky encompasses the earth, so he too encompasses the three worlds with his splendour. Endowed with food (water in the form of rain), he is the support of the heavenly world, just as he is the support of earth and sky.

##### English translation (H. H. Wilson)
Heaven and earth are insufficient for the girth of that Indra, who, with his greatness, rules over the leaders (of sacrifice) : as the atmosphere encompasses the earth, so he encompasses (the three worlds), and, being the master of the rain, he upholds the sky as well as the firmament and the earth.

##### विशेषविषयगळु (Special Topics)

महिना नृभ्यः प्र अस्ति—महत्वेन स्वैश्वर्येण नेतृभ्यः कर्मनिर्वाहकेभ्यः । यजमानेभ्यः प्रभवति । (*mahinā nṛbhyaḥ pra asti—mahatvena svaiśvaryeṇa netṛbhyaḥ karmanirvāhakebhyaḥ | yajamānebhyaḥ prabhavati |*). The meaning is explained thus: Indra is worshipful toward the sacrificers who perform the sacrificial rites, through his own majestic power (lordship) full of greatness. The word नरः (*naraḥ*) is stated among the names of the horse (Ni. 2-27) and among the names of man (Ni. 3-7). Here it is used in the sense of the sacrificers who perform the rites.

The word इत्था (*itthā*) is read among the names meaning "true", श्रत् (*śrat*) and so on (Ni. 3-13). In showing the nirvacana of the word इत्था (*itthā*), Yāska says इत्था अमुथेत्येतेन व्याख्यातं (*itthā amuthetyetena vyākhyātaṃ*) (Ni. 5-5): it is to be understood like the nirvacana of the word अमुथा (*amuthā*). अमुथा यथासाविति व्याख्यातं (*amuthā yathāsāviti vyākhyātaṃ*) (3-16): अमुथा (*amuthā*) is used in the sense of truth and also in the sense of comparison, and the word इत्था (*itthā*) is likewise used. Having shown this, he cites the Ṛk
त्यं चिदित्था कत्पयं शयानमसूर्ये शमसि वावृधानं । (*tyaṃ ciditthā katpayaṃ śayānamasūrye śamasi vāvṛdhānaṃ |*)

### Printed p. 272

(Ṛ. Saṃ. 5-32-6)
and shows that the word इत्था (*itthā*) means "truly" (Ni. 6-3). Durgācārya explains it as अमुना प्रकारेण (*amunā prakāreṇa*), and Sāyaṇa as अमुष्मिन्नंतरिक्षलोके (*amuṣminnaṃtarikṣaloke*). In the present Ṛk it is used in the sense of truth.

कक्ष्ये (*kakṣye*): कक्ष्या रज्जुरश्वस्य-कक्षं सेवते; कक्षो गाहतेः क्स इति नामकरणः । ख्यातीर्वानर्थकोऽभ्यासः । किमस्मिन् ख्यानमिति । कषतेर्वा । तत्सामान्यान्मनुष्यकक्षो बाहुमूलसामान्यादश्वस्य । (*kakṣyā rajjuraśvasya-kakṣaṃ sevate; kakṣo gāhateḥ ksa iti nāmakaraṇaḥ | khyātīrvānarthako 'bhyāsaḥ | kimasmin khyānamiti | kaṣatervā | tatsāmānyānmanuṣyakakṣo bāhumūlasāmānyādaśvasya |*) (Ni. 2-2). Because it is tied around the neck of the horse, the strap around its neck is called kakṣā. The affix क्स (*ksa*) joined with the root गाह् (*gāh*), used in the sense of entering within, may give the formation कक्ष्या (*kakṣyā*). Or it may be formed by reduplication of the root ख्या (*khyā*). Or the root कषा (*kaṣā*), meaning "to rub", may be its origin. Hence this name is also well known for the armpit (kakṣa) of a man; that is, heaven and earth, which have an interval equal to this armpit, may be meant.

Moreover, the Nirukta author has read the word कक्ष्या (*kakṣyā*) among the names of the "fingers" (Ni. 3-8), and he cites the Ṛk in which the word is used,
दशावनिभ्यो दशकक्ष्मेभ्यो दशयोक्रेभ्यो दशयोजनेभ्यः । दशाभीशुभ्यो अर्चताजरेभ्यो दश धुरो दश युक्ता वहद्भ्यः ॥ (*daśāvanibhyo daśakakṣmebhyo daśayokrebhyo daśayojanebhyaḥ | daśābhīśubhyo arcatājarebhyo daśa dhuro daśa yuktā vahadbhyaḥ ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-94),
and explains the word कक्ष्या (*kakṣyā*) there as fingers. In the present Ṛk it means the interval of heaven and earth.

अस्मै रोदसी कक्ष्ये नालं (*asmai rodasī kakṣye nālaṃ*): the word रोदसी (*rodasī*) is stated among the names of heaven and earth (Ni. 3-22). Although heaven and earth move along their own paths, they are not sufficient for the movement of Indra, who protects the sacrificers performing the rites. The meaning is explained: जगतोऽधीशस्य इंद्रस्य संचाराय अल्पत्वात् द्यावापृथिव्यौ न पर्याप्ते । सृष्टादपि स्रष्टुर्महिम्मो अधिकत्वात् । (*jagato 'dhīśasya iṃdrasya saṃcārāya alpatvāt dyāvāpṛthivyau na paryāpte | sṛṣṭādapi sraṣṭurmahimmo adhikatvāt |*). Since the greatness of the creator exceeds the greatness of the thing created, the room that heaven and earth afford is not sufficient for the movement of Indra, the lord of the whole world. This implies that the greatness of heaven and earth is less than the greatness of Indra.

आरं—अलं । पराप्त इत्यर्थः (*āraṃ—alaṃ | parāpta ityarthaḥ*). The meaning here is "sufficient, so as to give satisfaction". Besides giving the sense "sufficient", this word is also used in the senses "make ready" and "serve".

### Printed p. 273

वायवा याहि दर्ततेमे सोमा आरंकृताः । (*vāyavā yāhi dartateme somā āraṃkṛtāḥ |*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-2-1)
अरं कृण्वंतु वेदिं समग्निमिंधतां पुरः (*araṃ kṛṇvaṃtu vediṃ samagnimiṃdhatāṃ puraḥ*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-170-4)
In such Ṛks, आरंकृत (*āraṃkṛta*) and आरंकृण्वंतु (*āraṃkṛṇvaṃtu*) give the sense "make ready"; and in
त्वमग्ने प्रविणोदा आरंकृते त्वं देवः सविता रत्नधा असि । (*tvamagne praviṇodā āraṃkṛte tvaṃ devaḥ savitā ratnadhā asi |*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 2-1.7)
and similar Ṛks it gives the sense "worship, serve". In the present Ṛk, as in
सोमारुद्रा धारयेथामसुरं प्र वामिष्टयोऽरमश्नुवंतु । ( (*somārudrā dhārayethāmasuraṃ pra vāmiṣṭayo 'ramaśnuvaṃtu | (*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 6-74-1)
and similar Ṛks where it is used in the same way, it means "sufficient, so as to give satisfaction".

वृजनं न भूम संविव्ये-भूमिं भूतानि वा वृजनमिव । तदिव तव्यथा आवृणोति तथा आयमपि स्वतेजसा सम्यगावृणोति लोकत्रयं । (*vṛjanaṃ na bhūma saṃvivye-bhūmiṃ bhūtāni vā vṛjanamiva | tadiva tavyathā āvṛṇoti tathā āyamapi svatejasā samyagāvṛṇoti lokatrayaṃ |*). The word वृजन (*vṛjana*) is read among the names of strength (Ni. 3-9). The comparison is: as strength covers the earth and living beings. Here the word भूम (*bhūma*) is given the sense "earth" or "living beings". The meaning is described thus: as that strength covers the earth and the beings, so Indra covers the three worlds with his splendour.

स्वधावान् अन्नवान् (*svadhāvān annavān*) … or वृष्टिलक्षणोदकवान् वा (*vṛṣṭilakṣaṇodakavān vā*). The word स्वधा (*svadhā*) is read among the names of water (Ni. 2-24) and among the names of food (Ni. 3-9). The name is also used in yet other senses. Here the meaning is explained thus: Indra, possessing food and possessing waters in the form of rain, upholds the heavenly world.

Sāyaṇa has explained स्वधावान् (*svadhāvān*) as "possessing food, or possessing water in the form of rain". In some places Sāyaṇa himself explains the word स्वधया (*svadhayā*) in two ways: आत्मीयेन तेजसा; हनिर्लक्षणेन अन्नेन वा (*ātmīyena tejasā; hanirlakṣaṇena annena vā*). Since this is so, and since in the present Ṛk Indra's power is described as surpassing the greatness of heaven and earth, the meaning "possessing splendour" appears the more fitting here. The Nirukta authors have read this word together in the names of water (Ni. 2-23) and in the names of food (Ni. 3-9). In many contexts the word स्वधा (*svadhā*) is explained as one's own greatness, one's own splendour, or one's own support. For example—

### Printed p. 274

शश्वत्पुरोषा व्युवास देव्यथो अद्येदं व्यावो मघोनी । अथो व्युच्छादुत्तरा अनु द्यूनजरामृता चरति स्वधाभिः ॥ (*śaśvatpuroṣā vyuvāsa devyatho adyedaṃ vyāvo maghonī | atho vyucchāduttarā anu dyūnajarāmṛtā carati svadhābhiḥ ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-13-13)
In this Ṛk the word, used with reference to Ushas, is explained as आत्मीयैस्तेजोभिः (*ātmīyaistejobhiḥ*), "with her own splendours". Likewise in
यदंतरिक्षे पतथः पुरुभुजा यद्वेमे रोदसी अनु । यद्वा स्वधाभिरधितिष्ठथो रथमत आ यातमश्विना ॥ (*yadaṃtarikṣe patathaḥ purubhujā yadveme rodasī anu | yadvā svadhābhiradhitiṣṭhatho rathamata ā yātamaśvinā ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 8-10-6)
the explanation is आत्मीयैस्तेजोभिर्बलैर्वा सार्धं (*ātmīyaistejobhirbalairvā sārdhaṃ*): "together with your own splendours or with your own powers".

In the Ṛk
न मृत्युरासीदमृतं न तर्हि न रात्रा अह्न आसित्प्रकेतः । आनीदवातं स्वथया तवेकं तस्माद्धान्यन्न परः किं चनास ॥ (*na mṛtyurāsīdamṛtaṃ na tarhi na rātrā ahna āsitpraketaḥ | ānīdavātaṃ svathayā tavekaṃ tasmāddhānyanna paraḥ kiṃ canāsa ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-129-2)
Sāyaṇa has described स्वधा (*svadhā*), with the explanation स्वस्मिन् धीयते ध्रियते आश्रित्य वर्तते इति स्वधा (*svasmin dhīyate dhriyate āśritya vartate iti svadhā*)—"because it depends on itself for support"—as a qualifier of Māyā, the wondrous power of Īśvara (the Supreme Brahman). In the same way, in the present Ṛk too it may be explained that Indra has pervaded all the worlds with his unique, world-protecting splendour, greatness and power. Moreover, in
स्वधामनु श्रियं नशो महि श्लेषा अमवंतो वृषप्सवः । वहंते अह्रुतप्सवः । (*svadhāmanu śriyaṃ naśo mahi śleṣā amavaṃto vṛṣapsavaḥ | vahaṃte ahrutapsavaḥ |*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 8-20-7)
वि विद्युतो न वृष्टिभी रुचाना अनु स्वधा मायुधैरच्छमानाः । (*vi vidyuto na vṛṣṭibhī rucānā anu svadhā māyudhairacchamānāḥ |*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 7-56-13)
and similar Ṛks, where the expressions स्वधां अनु (*svadhāṃ anu*) and अनु स्वधां (*anu svadhāṃ*) occur, the meanings are "naturally", "by nature", "according to custom".

In
अचक्रया यत्स्वधया सुपर्णो हव्यं भरन्मनवे देवजुष्टं । (*acakrayā yatsvadhayā suparṇo havyaṃ bharanmanave devajuṣṭaṃ |*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 4-26-4)
it is explained as "by the chariot", and in
अपश्यं ग्रामं वहमानमारादचक्रया स्वधया वर्तमानं । (*apaśyaṃ grāmaṃ vahamānamārādacakrayā svadhayā vartamānaṃ |*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-27-19)

### Printed p. 275

and similar places as "by the army". On the whole, it indicates the nature of the thing as "endowed with splendour". It may be said that in the present Ṛk the word स्वधावान् (*svadhāvān*) describes the power of the supremely glorious Indra.

ओपशमिव—ईषदुपशेते इत्योपतं शृंगं तद्वृषभ इव । (*opaśamiva—īṣadupaśete ityopataṃ śṛṃgaṃ tadvṛṣabha iva |*). Because it arises to a small extent, the horn is given as the meaning of the word ओपश (*opaśa*). In regard to Indra's upholding the three worlds, one kind of meaning is explained by comparing him to a bull (ox) that bears effortlessly a horn that has grown to a small extent. In the second meaning: पतस्पतं समीपे वर्तमानं क्षित्यंतरिक्षाख्यं लोकत्वयं तडिव ह्यां बिभर्ति । (*pataspataṃ samīpe vartamānaṃ kṣityaṃtarikṣākhyaṃ lokatvayaṃ taḍiva hyāṃ bibharti |*) **NOTE** लोकत्रयमपि बिभर्तित्यर्थः (*lokatrayamapi bibhartityarthaḥ*). The comparison is: as one upholds the two worlds, well known as earth and atmosphere, which lie close to each other, so Indra upholds the heavenly world. It is thereby made clear that Indra's abundant greatness, by which he effortlessly protects the three worlds, is described.

तोषसी (*toṣasī*)—**NOTE** the word means heaven and earth. As a proper name, the word also denotes in many contexts a person (a female attendant). Hence the word is used both in the singular and in the dual; its meaning is to be taken according to each context. The Nirukta author, citing the Ṛk
उताभये पुरुहूत श्रवोभिरेको दृळहमवदो वृत्रहा सन् । इमे चिदिंद्र रोदसी अपारे यत्संगृभ्णा मघवन् काशिरित्ते ॥ (*utābhaye puruhūta śravobhireko dṛḷahamavado vṛtrahā san | ime cidiṃdra rodasī apāre yatsaṃgṛbhṇā maghavan kāśiritte ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 3-30-5)
and stating the nirvacana of the word रोदसी (*rodasī*) there, says
रोदसी द्यावापृथिव्य विरोधनात् (*rodasī dyāvāpṛthivya virodhanāt*)
(Ni. 6-1).
It is to be understood that heaven and earth are well known by the name रोदसी (*rodasī*) because they are bound and restrained by a limited boundary (sīmā). This is the nirvacana of the dual form रोदसी (*rodasī*). Again, giving the meaning of the singular form, he explains
कोदसी रुद्रस्य पत्नि (*kodasī rudrasya patni*)
(Ni. 11-49):
Rodasī is the wife of Rudra; and then cites
रथं नु मारुतं वयं श्रवस्युमा हुवामहे । आ यस्मिन्तस्थौ सुरणानि बिभ्रती सचा मरुत्सु रोदसी ॥ (*rathaṃ nu mārutaṃ vayaṃ śravasyumā huvāmahe | ā yasmintasthau suraṇāni bibhratī sacā marutsu rodasī ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 5-56-8).

### Printed p. 276

Citing this Ṛk, he shows that here the proper name Rodasī, "wife of Rudra", is used. But another question arises here. Sāyaṇa has explained in two places in a way that leaves room for doubt: is "wife of Rudra" the wife of the deity Rudra, or does the word Rudra mean Marut, so that she is the wife of the Marut deities? Explaining the present Ṛk, he says
कोदसी रुद्रस्य पत्नि मरुतां माता । यद्वा रुद्रो वायुः । तत्पत्नी माध्यमिका देवी ॥ (*kodasī rudrasya patni marutāṃ mātā | yadvā rudro vāyuḥ | tatpatnī mādhyamikā devī ‖*)
(Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya).
One meaning may be that Rodasī is the wife of Rudra and the mother of the Maruts. Or, since the word Rudra has the meaning Vāyu, Rodasī is the Mādhyamikā Vāk, the wife of Vāyu: thus he has explained it in two ways. And in explaining the Ṛk
परा शुभ्रा अयासो यव्या साधारण्येव मरुतो मिमिक्षुः । न रोदसी अप नुदंत घोरा जुषंत वृधं सख्याय देवाः ॥ (*parā śubhrā ayāso yavyā sādhāraṇyeva maruto mimikṣuḥ | na rodasī apa nudaṃta ghorā juṣaṃta vṛdhaṃ sakhyāya devāḥ ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-167-4)
he says, as stated earlier,
रोदसा शब्देन रोदनस्वभावो रुद्रः । तस्य स्त्री रोदसीति केचिदाहुः । आदरे तु मरुतां स्त्रीयो रोदसीति नामधेयमित्याहुः । अयमेव पक्षो युक्त उत्तरत्रैवं व्यवहारात् । (*rodasā śabdena rodanasvabhāvo rudraḥ | tasya strī rodasīti kecidāhuḥ | ādare tu marutāṃ strīyo rodasīti nāmadheyamityāhuḥ | ayameva pakṣo yukta uttaratraivaṃ vyavahārāt |*).
That Rodasī is the wife of Rudra is one view; that she is the wife of the Maruts is another. Of these, the second appears to be the correct one: thus he has stated his own opinion. Besides, in explaining the Ṛk
जोषद्यदीमसुर्य्या सचध्यै विषितस्तुका रोदसी नृमणाः ॥ (*joṣadyadīmasuryyā sacadhyai viṣitastukā rodasī nṛmaṇāḥ ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-167-5)
he has stated रोदसी मरुत्पत्नी विद्युद्वा (*rodasī marutpatnī vidyudvā*): Rodasī is the wife of the Maruts, or lightning is known by this name; and he has cited the subject described there, that this Rodasī is in the following of the Maruts. And if we examine some further contexts in which the word रोदसी (*rodasī*) is used,
त इदुग्राः तवसा धृष्णुषेणा उभे युजंत रोदसी सुमेके । अथ स्मैषु रोदसी स्वशोचिरामवत्सु तस्थौ न रोकः ॥ (*ta idugrāḥ tavasā dhṛṣṇuṣeṇā ubhe yujaṃta rodasī sumeke | atha smaiṣu rodasī svaśocirāmavatsu tasthau na rokaḥ ‖*)
(Ṛ. Saṃ. 6-66-6)
in this one Ṛk the word रोदसी (*rodasī*) is used in two senses. Here Sāyaṇa has explained the first occurrence as व्यावापृथिव्य (*vyāvāpṛthivya*) and the second as रुद्रस्य पत्नि माध्यमिकी वाक्य (*rudrasya patni mādhyamikī vākya*). And,

### Printed p. 277
and,

मिम्मक्ष येषु रोदसी नु देवी सिषक्ति पूषा अभ्यर्धयज्वा । श्रुत्वा हवं मरुतो यद्ध याथ भूमा रेजंते अध्वनि प्रविक्ते ॥ (*mimmakṣa yeṣu rodasī nu devī siṣakti pūṣā abhyardhayajvā | śrutvā havaṃ maruto yaddha yātha bhūmā rejaṃte adhvani pravikte ‖*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 6-50-5).

Here too, देवी (*devī*) is explained as the shining speech of the middle region (the thunder). Giving the explanation of Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-167-3, the scholar Max Muller describes the word रोदसी (*rodasī*), used in the senses of lightning, the speech of the middle region, and heaven-and-earth, as follows.

In our verse, we take वाक् (*vāk*) in the sense of thunder, but as a
feminine, of the lightning and thunder as the two companions of
the Maruts, represents the first, रोदसी (*rodasī*) or lightning, as the
recognised wife, hiding herself in the house, while the other, the
loud thunder, is represented as a more public companion of
the Maurts. This contrast, if it is really what the poet intended,
throws a curious light on the social character of the Vedic times,
as it presupposes two classes of wives, not necessarily simultanious,
however—, a house wife, who stays at home and is not much seen,
and a wife who appears in public and takes part in the society........
The loud voice of the thuuder as well as the usual hiding of the
lightning might well suggest this comparison.
[S. B. E. XXXII P. 276]

Pointing out in this way the difference between the singular and the dual use, the Bṛhaddevatā says:

रोदसी देवपत्नीनां अथर्वांगिरसे यथा । (*rodasī devapatnīnāṃ atharvāṃgirase yathā |*) (Bṛ. De. 2-143)

Here is an example of the singular use: according to the view of the Atharvāṅgirasa (Atharvaveda), Rodasī is one of the wives of the gods. And,

द्वे द्वे पदे संस्तुते रोदसी च देवाश्चार्थर्चेन विभक्तमन्यत् । (*dve dve pade saṃstute rodasī ca devāścārtharcena vibhaktamanyat |*) (Bṛ. De. 4-6)

In all such places where the dual is used, heaven and earth are meant.

##### Grammar

- महिना — मह पूजायां धातुः । इन् सर्वधातुभ्यः इति इन् प्रत्ययः । तृतीया एकवचनांतरूपं । व्यत्ययेन विभक्तेः उदात्तस्वरः भवति ।

### Printed p. 278
- आस्ति — यद्योगात् निघातस्वरः न भवति । तिप् पित्त्वात् धातुस्वरः अवशिष्यते ।
- अरम् — नालमूललघु-(पा. म. ८-२-१८) इति लकारस्य रेफादेशः ।
- विव्ये — वी गत्यादिषु धातुः । छंदसि लुज्लज्लिटः इति वर्तमानार्थे लिट् । प्रथमपुरुष एकवचनांतरूपं । तिजंतनिघातस्वरः भवति ।
- वृजनम् — वृजी वर्जने धातुः । अत्र कर्तर्यर्थे प्रयुक्तः । कृष्यवृजिमंदि—(उ. सू. २-२३९) इति क्यु प्रत्ययः । युवोरनाकौ (पा सू. ७-१-१) इति तस्य अनादेशः । कित्त्वात् लघूपधगुणः न भवति । प्रत्ययस्य आद्युदात्तस्वरः भवति ।
- भर्ति — भृञ् भरणे धातुः । लट् प्रथमपुरुष एकवचने तिप् । बहुलं छंदसि इति शपः लुक् । सार्वधातुकार्थधातुकयोः इति इकः गुणः । र परत्वेन भवति । पादादौ इति निघातस्वरः न भवति । तिप् पित्त्वात् अनुदात्तत्वात् धातुस्वरः अवशिष्यते ।
- स्वधावान् — माधुपधायाश्च—इति मतुपः मकारस्य वत्वं । दीर्घादटि समानपादे (पा.सू. ८-३-९) इति नकारस्य रुत्वं । आतोटि नित्यम् (पा. सू. ८-३-३) इति सूत्रेण नित्यं आकारस्य अनुनासिकत्वं भवति ।
- ओपशम् — शीञ् स्वप्ने धातुः । आज्, उप, द्वौ उपसर्गौ उपपदत्वे बहुलग्रहणात् अस्मात् ड प्रत्ययः । डित्त्वात् टिलोपः । कृदुत्तरपदप्रकृतिस्वरः भवति ॥ ६ ॥

#### Ṛk 7

##### ॥ संहितापाठः ॥ (Saṃhitā-pāṭha)

> स॒मत्सु॑ त्वा शूर स॒तामु॑रा॒णं प्र॑प॒थिंत॑मं परितंस॒यध्यै॑ ।  
> स॒जोष॑स॒ इंद्रं॒ मदे॑ क्षो॒णीः सू॒रिं चि॒द्ये अ॑नु॒मदं॑ति॒ वाज्यैः॑ ॥

##### ॥ पदपाठः ॥ (Pada-pāṭha)

> समत्सु । त्वा । शूर । सतां । उराणं । प्रपथिन्तमं । परितंसयध्यै ।  
> सजोषसः । इंद्रं । मदे । क्षोणीः । सूरिं । चित् । ये । अनुमदंति । वाज्यैः ॥

### Printed p. 279

##### ॥ सायणभाष्यम् ॥ (Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya)

> ४ शारदाभ्युदय ॥
> हे तूर शौर्योपेतेंद्र समत्सु संग्रामेषु सतां त्वामेवाश्रित्य
> वर्तमानानां प्राणिनामुराणमुरूण्यतिप्रभूतानि बलादीनि कुर्वाणं प्रपथिंतमं
> प्रकृष्टमार्गतमं सुकृतिनामुत्तममार्गभूतमिंद्रं शतृणां दारकं त्वा त्वां
> परितंसयध्या अवतंसीकर्तुं भूषणीकर्तुं । भूषयितुं वा निमित्तेऽर्थे ।
> मदे तव मदार्थं च सजोषसः सह सेवमानाः समानप्रीतयो वा क्षोणीः
> क्षोण्यो विशः परिजना मरुतः प्रयतंते । कीदृशास्ते । ये वाजैर्बलैरनुमदंति
> अनुकूलं मादयंति संग्रामे मरुतस्तमेव स्वामिनं कृत्वा मादयंति ।
> हर्षयंतीत्यर्थः । यद्वा । ये यजमाना वाजैर्हविर्भिमादयंति ते
> समानप्रीतियुक्तास्तव मनुष्यभूता ऋत्विजो यजमानभूताः प्रजाः समत्सु
> सहवादनस्थानेषु यागेषूक्तलक्षणविशिष्टं त्वामेव पूजयितुं तव मादाय च
> प्रायतंते ॥

##### Gist of the bhāṣya

Addressing Indra as शूर (*śūra*), Sāyaṇa says: in battles (समत्सु (*samatsu*)) you cause very abundant strength and the like (उरूणि अतिप्रभूतानि बलादीनि (*urūṇi atiprabhūtāni balādīni*)) for the creatures who live relying on you; you are प्रपथिंतमं (*prapathiṃtamaṃ*), the best of paths for the virtuous, and Indra, the render of enemies. The Maruts, here called क्षोणीः (*kṣoṇīḥ*) (his retinue, the people) and सजोषसः (*sajoṣasaḥ*) (serving together, or of equal affection), strive to adorn you (परितंसयध्यै (*paritaṃsayadhyai*): to make you an ornament, or to decorate you) and for your exhilaration (मदे (*made*)). Who are they? Those who (ये (*ye*)) with their strengths (वाजैः (*vājaiḥ*)) gladden you favourably (अनुमदंति (*anumadaṃti*)): in battle the Maruts, making him alone their lord, delight him. Alternatively: the sacrificers who gladden you with oblations (वाजैः (*vājaiḥ*)), united in affection, being your human priests and sacrificers, your subjects, strive in the sacrifices (समत्सु (*samatsu*), places of joint rejoicing) to honour you, who have the stated qualities, and for your exhilaration.

##### प्रतिपदार्थ (Pratipadārtha)

- हे शूर (*he śūra*)—O heroic Indra
- ये (*ye*)—which Marut gods
- वाजैः (*vājaiḥ*)—with their own strength
- सूरिं चित् (*sūriṃ cit*)—you, the wise one
- अनु मदंति (*anu madaṃti*)—follow (so as to be favourable to you) and cause delight
- ते (*te*)—those
- क्षोणीः (*kṣoṇīḥ*)—the retinue (Maruts)
- सजोषसः (*sajoṣasaḥ*)—serving together
- समत्सु (*samatsu*)—in battles
- सतां (*satāṃ*)—for the creatures who rely on you
- उराणं (*urāṇaṃ*)—the giver of abundant strength
- प्रपथिंतमं (*prapathiṃtamaṃ*)—the best path (for the doers of good deeds)
- इंद्रं (*iṃdraṃ*)—the destroyer of enemies
- त्वा (*tvā*)—you
- परितंसयध्यॆ (*paritaṃsayadhyè*)—to adorn (and)
- मदे (*made*)—for your delight (they strive)
- Or: हे शूर (*he śūra*)—O heroic Indra; ये (*ye*)—which sacrificers; वाजैः (*vājaiḥ*)—with oblations and the like; सूरिं, चित् (*sūriṃ, cit*)—you, the wise one; अनु मदंति (*anu madaṃti*)—gladden; ते (*te*)—those; क्षोणीः (*kṣoṇīḥ*)—the sacrificers, who are like the retinue; सजोषसः (*sajoṣasaḥ*)—of equal affection; सतां उराणं (*satāṃ urāṇaṃ*)—the strength-giver to those who rely on you; प्रपथिंतमं (*prapathiṃtamaṃ*)—the best path; इंद्रं (*iṃdraṃ*)—the destroyer of enemies; त्वा (*tvā*)—you; परितंसयध्यॆ (*paritaṃsayadhyè*)—in order to worship; and मदे (*made*)—for your delight; समत्सु (*samatsu*)—in sacrifices, the places of delight (they strive).

##### भावार्थ (Bhāvārtha)

O heroic Indra, you who give strength to those who rely on you, are the best path for the doers of good deeds, and destroy enemies: the Maruts (or the sacrificers) strive with their own strength to worship you and to delight you.

### Printed p. 280

##### English translation (H. H. Wilson)
Those people, who, jointly worshipping Indra, diligently endeavour,
O hero, to propitiate you, the invigorator in combats, the guide in the
rightway : provide, for his gratification, with sacrificial viands.

##### विशेषविषयगळु (Special Topics)

In this Ṛk the unique help of the Maruts, who always give and will give Indra help in times of battle, is described. In the same way it is also explained how the sacrificers who perform sacrifices strive to worship Indra in the sacrifices.

समत्सु सतां उराणं (*samatsu satāṃ urāṇaṃ*)—Here Indra is addressed by the word शूर (*śūra*), and the meaning is explained thus: in times of battle Indra graciously grants that even the creatures who rely on him obtain surpassing ability and the like (उरूणि अतिप्रभूतानि बलादीनि कुर्वाणं (*urūṇi atiprabhūtāni balādīni kurvāṇaṃ*)). The word समत्सु (*samatsu*) is listed among the names of battle (Ni. 3-9). And the Nirukta author, giving the derivation of this word, states the alternatives:

समहः समदो वात्तॆः; संमदो वा मदतेः । (*samahaḥ samado vāttèḥ; saṃmado vā madateḥ |*) (Ni. 9-17)

That is, the prefix सम् (*sam*) joined with the root अद् (*ad*) may become समद् (*samad*); or the prefix सम् (*sam*) joined with the root मद् (*mad*), meaning to be excited, to be roused with ardour, may become समद् (*samad*). He cites the Ṛk in which the word समत्सु (*samatsu*) is used,

आ जंघंति सान्वेषां जघना उप जिघ्नते । अश्वाजनि प्रचेतसोऽश्वान्समत्तु चोदय ॥ (*ā jaṃghaṃti sānveṣāṃ jaghanā upa jighnate | aśvājani pracetaso 'śvānsamattu codaya ‖*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 6-75-13)

and explains there the special sense of this word.

प्रपंचितमं—सुकृतिनामुत्तममार्गभूतं (*prapaṃcitamaṃ—sukṛtināmuttamamārgabhūtaṃ*): since Indra shows the best path to the virtuous, he is stated, by the identification of cause and effect, to be the best path itself.

इंद्रं-शत्रुणां दारकं (*iṃdraṃ-śatruṇāṃ dārakaṃ*): the Nirukta authors derive the word इंद्र (*iṃdra*) in many ways. Of these Sāyaṇa follows इंदर् शत्रुणां दारयिता (*iṃdar śatruṇāṃ dārayitā*) (Ni. 10-8), "Indra is the one who splits enemies", and so describes him here as the destroyer of enemies.

### Printed p. 281
सजोषसः क्षोणीः—सह सेवमानाः समानप्रीतयो ना क्षोण्यो विशः परिजनाः । (*sajoṣasaḥ kṣoṇīḥ—saha sevamānāḥ samānaprītayo nā kṣoṇyo viśaḥ parijanāḥ |*) These two words are derived and explained as epithets of the Maruts. Although the Maruts are many (एकोन पंचाशत्संख्याकाः (*ekona paṃcāśatsaṃkhyākāḥ*)—forty-nine persons), they are in affection through unity among themselves, or all of them serve Indra with one and the same affection; and though they are like Indra's friends, they are like subjects. This is the meaning explained.

ये वाजैः अनु मदंति (*ye vājaiḥ anu madaṃti*)—This sentence is explained in two ways, with regard to the Maruts and with regard to the sacrificers. First: मरुतस्तमेव स्वामिनं कृत्वा संग्रामे बलैः हर्षयंति । (*marutastameva svāminaṃ kṛtvā saṃgrāme balaiḥ harṣayaṃti |*) The Maruts, knowing Indra alone as lord, delight him in times of battle with their strength. In this sense the word वाज (*vāja*) is used in the meaning of strength. Yāska lists the word वाज (*vāja*) among the names of food (Ni. 3-9), among the names of battle (Ni. 3-9), and as the name of a person (Ni. 11-17), and on the Ṛk

सावका नः स्वरस्वती वाजेभिर्वाजिनीवती । यज्ञं वष्टु धियावसुः । (*sāvakā naḥ svarasvatī vājebhirvājinīvatī | yajñaṃ vaṣṭu dhiyāvasuḥ |*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-3-10)

he explains the meaning as oblations or water (हविषोदकेन (*haviṣodakena*)).**NOTE**

Second: ये यजमानाः (*ye yajamānāḥ*), the sacrificers who perform sacrifices, वाजैः हविर्भिर्मादयंति ते समानप्रितियुक्ताः तव मनुष्यभूता ऋत्विजः यजमानभूताः प्रजाः समत्तु सहमादनस्थानेषु यागेषु उक्तलक्षणविशिष्टं त्वामेव पूजयितुं तव मादाय च प्रयतंते । (*vājaiḥ havirbhirmādayaṃti te samānapritiyuktāḥ tava manuṣyabhūtā ṛtvijaḥ yajamānabhūtāḥ prajāḥ samattu sahamādanasthāneṣu yāgeṣu uktalakṣaṇaviśiṣṭaṃ tvāmeva pūjayituṃ tava mādāya ca prayataṃte |*) United in a single affection towards Indra, they delight him with oblations. Being in human form both priests and sacrificers, Indra's subjects, in the sacrifices, which are occasions that cause delight to Indra and to themselves, they worship Indra, who is endowed with abundant greatness, and strive to cause him delight. This is the meaning explained.

The word समत्सु (*samatsu*) is listed among the names of battle (Ni. 3-9). Here Sāyaṇa takes the word in the sense of सहमादनस्थानेषु यागेषु (*sahamādanasthāneṣu yāgeṣu*), the sacrifice, an occasion which increases exhilaration in all at one time, and so explains the meaning.

Sāyaṇa has not explained the word सूरिंजित् (*sūriṃjit*) in this Ṛk.

सूरयः (*sūrayaḥ*)—the Nirukta authors read this word among the 13 names of praisers, रेभः, जरिता, कारुः (*rebhaḥ, jaritā, kāruḥ*) and so on (Ni. 3-19). सूरि (*sūri*) may mean the devotee who praises, or it may mean wise, discerning, intelligent. When it is used as an epithet of the seers who have the supernatural power of vision, both of the eye and of the mind, which grasps the true natures of the gods and the secret principles of the universe,

### Printed p. 282
the word has a special sense.

तद्विष्टो ४ पॆरमं पवं सदा पश्यंति सूरयः । (*tadviṣṭo 4 pèramaṃ pavaṃ sadā paśyaṃti sūrayaḥ |*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-22-20)

In such contexts it does not merely mean those who praise, but also indicates the seers' extraordinary power of vision. And it is used as an epithet not only of seers but also of the gods, Indra and others.

मंहिष्ठमच्छोक्तिभिर्मतीनां सुवृक्तिभिः सूरिं वावृधध्यै । (*maṃhiṣṭhamacchoktibhirmatīnāṃ suvṛktibhiḥ sūriṃ vāvṛdhadhyai |*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-81-3)

In Ṛks such as this, with विपश्चितमिंद्रं (*vipaścitamiṃdraṃ*), it shows the wisdom of the gods, Indra and others. Besides, the word सूरि (*sūri*) is also used when the meaning lord, impeller, or leader on the good path is indicated. And as examples,

जनो यः पज्रेभ्यो वाजिनीवानश्वावतो रथिनो मह्यं सूरिः । (*jano yaḥ pajrebhyo vājinīvānaśvāvato rathino mahyaṃ sūriḥ |*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-122-8)

असद्यथा जरित्र उत सूरिरिंद्रो रायो विश्ववारस्य दाता । (*asadyathā jaritra uta sūririṃdro rāyo viśvavārasya dātā |*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 6-23-10)

मुह्यंत्वन्ये अभितो जनास इहास्माकं मघवा सूरिरस्तु । (*muhyaṃtvanye abhito janāsa ihāsmākaṃ maghavā sūrirastu |*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 10-81-6)

In these and similar contexts this epithet is applied to gods who are lords and leaders on the good path, and describes their quality.

आदस्य ते कृष्णासो दक्षि सूरयः शूरस्येव श्वेषथादीषते वयः । (*ādasya te kṛṣṇāso dakṣi sūrayaḥ śūrasyeva śveṣathādīṣate vayaḥ |*) (Ṛ. Saṃ. 1-141-8)

In such contexts it also gives the meaning of path. In the present Ṛk it may be explained as wise or lord.

सजोषसः क्षोणीः (*sajoṣasaḥ kṣoṇīḥ*)—"those of equal affection" and "those who are like retinue" indicate the eternal and close relationship between Indra and the Maruts. This relationship was explained earlier in this same volume, in the Special Topics of Ṛk 1-171-4. It is because of such companionship that, on all occasions when Indra accompanied by the Maruts is addressed, Indra is called मरुत्वान् (*marutvān*); so the Bṛhaddevatā explains this epithet that indicates the relationship of these deities.

मरुद्भिः सह यत्रेंद्रो मरुत्वांस्तत्र सोऽभवत् । (*marudbhiḥ saha yatreṃdro marutvāṃstatra so 'bhavat |*) (Bṛ. De. 4-56)
